TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

Trump’s trip kicks off in Manila, Philippines, where he will join leaders of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN) on Tuesday to discuss some of the greatest threats to the region, including how wars in Iran and Ukraine have impacted the bloc’s economic security . During that time, Trump will also hold in-person talks with his fellow Quadrilateral Security Dialogue leaders—Australian Prime Minister Anthony Albanese, Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi, and Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi—for the first time since taking office nearly two years ago. Meeting with the Quad will offer several opportunities for pomp and pageantry; however, experts expect little substance to come out of the summit. “I don’t think the president can hide the fact that he’s not terribly enthusiastic about the gathering,” Kurt Campbell, the co-founder of the Asia Group, a Washington-based geopolitical advisory firm, told FP’s Rishi Iyengar. Instead, the talks will likely serve as a box-checking exercise before Trump can turn his attention to other matters—namely, China. After less than 24 hours in the Philippines, Trump will head to the Chinese city of Shenzhen for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit. * The Middle East Is Finally on Its Own
  Foreign Policy | 16 Nov 20:49 UTC
  The Iran war is bringing a century of regional intervention by great powers to a close.
  FULL TEXT: According to Steve Simon , Washington’s dreams of “transforming” the Middle East have failed in the past, are failing today, and, if we continue them, will keep failing. He’s right, but I don’t think that we will continue these efforts, and neither will anyone else. On the contrary, we are approaching the end of a century or more in which outside powers drove events in the region even when they couldn’t achieve their main goals. For the first time since 1919, what happens there will be primarily determined by the local actors themselves. The failed U.S.-Israeli war against Iran is a watershed event, but it is merely accelerating developments that have been apparent for some time. Increasingly, states in the Middle East are going to be on their own. The modern Middle East was heavily shaped by outside great powers. The victorious World War I allies carved new states out of the collapsing Ottoman Empire at the Paris Peace Conference in 1919, after making a lot of contradictory promises to various contenders during the war itself. Britain and France governed what is now Lebanon, Iraq, Israel, Syria, and Jordan for most of the interwar period, under mandates from the League of Nations, but the local inhabitants were increasingly restive even before World War II broke out and made it impossible for European powers to retain control. Saudi Arabia gained independence on its own, while Britain provided security for the smaller states in the Gulf. Active support from various outside powers—and especially the United States—was instrumental in Israel’s creation in 1948. Britain and the United States connived to topple the democratic government of Iran in 1953. And the Soviet decision to send arms to Egypt and Syria in the mid-1950s helped cause the 1956 Suez crisis and made the Middle East a central arena in the Cold War. According to Steve Simon , Washington’s dreams of “transforming” the Middle East have failed in the past, are failing today, and, if we continue them, will keep failing. He’s right, but I don’t think that we will continue these efforts, and neither will anyone else. On the contrary, we are approaching the end of a century or more in which outside powers drove events in the region even when they couldn’t achieve their main goals. For the first time since 1919, what happens there will be primarily determined by the local actors themselves.   https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/16/united-states-middle-east-intervention-era-over/

Perhaps for that reason, on Monday, Trump told reporters he “would not mind” if Huang was reinstated. * Japan’s Defense Lessons From Ukraine and the Middle East
  Foreign Policy | 16 Nov 16:08 UTC
  The ability to surge weapons production in wartime is now a critical pillar of deterrence.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Since taking office in October 2025, Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi has been busy reforming Japan’s defense policy. Her government is revising the National Security Strategy , National Defense Strategy , and Defense Buildup Program to prepare for an era of heightened instability and growing uncertainty over U.S. engagement in the region. Notably, Tokyo raised defense and other security-related spending to approximately 1.8 percent of GDP in the 2025-2026fiscal year, up from around 1 percent in 2022. In April, Japan also revised its defense transfer policy with the aim of promoting exports of weapons and nonlethal military equipment. Expanding Japan’s defense production capacity has been a central focus of these reforms. In its budget request for the 2027 fiscal year, the Japanese Defense Ministry singles out domestic military production as a critical element of deterrence. This reflects growing concerns that Japan may need to sustain military operations in a protracted, high-intensity conflict. In an analysis of the U.S.-Israel war against Iran that was published in August, the Defense Ministry explicitly recognizes the need to maintain adequate production capacity in peacetime—and to be ready to rapidly scale up weapons production in the event of a conflict. * How Xi Sees a Weakened Trump
  Foreign Policy | 16 Nov 12:00 UTC
  Political instability is more danger than opportunity for Beijing.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Voters dealt U.S. President Donald Trump a crushing rebuke in the Nov. 3 midterm elections, with Republicans losing control of both chambers of Congress. As Trump meets once again with Xi Jinping this week, how will the Chinese leader now view a weakened U.S. president? I believe Xi will not fundamentally change his personal assessment of Trump. But he is reassessing Trump’s ability to shape U.S.-China relations over the next two years—and the extent to which the two leaders can deliver on the new framework for bilateral relations they agreed on this year. Beijing may come to see Trump as a president who remains willing to negotiate on a personal level but is becoming less capable of delivering on major political commitments. Voters dealt U.S. President Donald Trump a crushing rebuke in the Nov. 3 midterm elections, with Republicans losing control of both chambers of Congress. As Trump meets once again with Xi Jinping this week, how will the Chinese leader now view a weakened U.S. president? I believe Xi will not fundamentally change his personal assessment of Trump. But he is reassessing Trump’s ability to shape U.S.-China relations over the next two years—and the extent to which the two leaders can deliver on the new framework for bilateral relations they agreed on this year. Beijing may come to see Trump as a president who remains willing to negotiate on a personal level but is becoming less capable of delivering on major political commitments. This matters because both Xi and Trump place considerable importance on personal diplomacy between heads of state. Trump visited China in May, and Xi traveled to the United States in September. During those meetings, according to the Chinese readout, they agreed to establish a new framework for bilateral relations: a “constructive and strategically stable relationship based on respect, fairness, and reciprocity.” Xi also called for a relationship characterized by “cooperation as the priority, managed competition, controlled differences, and the prospect of peace.” Trump, for his part, repeatedly emphasized his good personal relationship with Xi and expressed confidence that U.S.-China relations could succeed under their leadership. Despite their very different political backgrounds and governing philosophies, Xi and Trump, who have both sought to expand their own authority at home, share a belief that strong leaders can play a decisive role in national decision-making. * Democrats Can’t Return to Pre-Trump Foreign Policy
  Foreign Policy | 16 Nov 12:00 UTC
  The world has changed. A new approach must take that into account.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. With Democrats in decisive control of the 120th Congress, the time has come for the party to offer a vision for what comes next for U.S. foreign policy. The country’s stunning repudiation of U.S. President Donald Trump is stoking the temptation to embrace a sharp policy reversal, on the assumption that the center of American democracy has held and the pre-Trump foreign-policy consensus can, too. However, when it comes to working with U.S. allies, that would be a grave mistake. The next Congress has an important role to play in defining this new foreign-policy vision—one that the next president could build upon in 2029. In asserting its powers to conduct oversight, legislate, and conduct its own diplomacy, the Democratic majority should be seeking to put in place elements of a new U.S. approach, rather than simply reverse the Trumpian damage. Doing so will begin to reorient U.S. foreign policy in the next two years, shape foreign-policy debates in the coming presidential primary, and allow the next commander in chief to confront the world as it will be in 2029. * Can South Africa Tackle HIV Without the U.S.?
  Foreign Policy | 16 Nov 10:00 UTC
  The rollout of a groundbreaking drug is an early test in a post-PEPFAR world.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Two years ago, Linda-Gail Bekker was jubilant. She had just led a major clinical trial in South Africa, the epicenter of the global HIV epidemic, testing a new form of prevention that was astonishingly effective. The drug, lenacapavir, is a pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP), which blocks the virus from spreading in HIV-negative people. A twice-yearly injection could replace daily pills, offering longer-lasting protection and thus slowing the spread of the virus. When Bekker, the CEO of the Desmond Tutu Health Foundation in Cape Town, South Africa, announced the results of her trial at the International AIDS Conference in Munich in July 2024, the room erupted in a standing ovation . “There was huge anticipation and excitement. And then January came around, and suddenly that all disappeared,” she said, referring to the start of U.S. President Donald Trump’s second term. Two years ago, Linda-Gail Bekker was jubilant. She had just led a major clinical trial in South Africa, the epicenter of the global HIV epidemic, testing a new form of prevention that was astonishingly effective. The drug, lenacapavir, is a pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP), which blocks the virus from spreading in HIV-negative people. A twice-yearly injection could replace daily pills, offering longer-lasting protection and thus slowing the spread of the virus. When Bekker, the CEO of the Desmond Tutu Health Foundation in Cape Town, South Africa, announced the results of her trial at the International AIDS Conference in Munich in July 2024, the room erupted in a standing ovation . “There was huge anticipation and excitement. And then January came around, and suddenly that all disappeared,” she said, referring to the start of U.S. President Donald Trump’s second term. The Trump administration took a chainsaw to global health programs, including the U.S. President’s Emergency Plan for AIDS Relief (PEPFAR), which has provided around $8 billion to South Africa since 2003. South Africa pays for most of its own HIV programs, including 90 percent of its antiretroviral drugs. Still, the collapse of PEPFAR was devastating , shuttering clinics for high-risk groups and leaving thousands of health workers without jobs. South Africa is home to around 8 million people living with HIV, or around 20 percent of the global HIV population. Approximately 6 million of them are on treatment, but the country still records around 170,000 new infections each year. When South Af
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/16/south-africa-hiv-aids-pepfar-trump-health-funding-withdrawal/

--- Editorial (38) ---

* Somali Women and Children From Roj Camp Held in Damascus
  New Lines | 16 Nov 19:22 UTC
  Authorities removed the 33 women and children from Roj camp and are holding them in poor conditions in a basement cell in Damascus, where they face possible deportation to Somalia. The post Somali Women and Children From Roj Camp Held in Damascus appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: (Photo: Interior of the basement room in Damascus.) A group of 33 Somali women and children, who were recently removed from a camp for families of alleged Islamic State group sympathizers, has been detained by Syrian authorities in a basement cell underneath the former Immigration and Passports Department in central Damascus, according to documents and information reviewed by New Lines Magazine . The facility had been an official building but was partially burned during the takeover of Damascus by rebel forces, led by Hayat Tahrir al-Sham, in December 2024. On Nov. 8, Syrian government officials took the individuals from Roj camp, according to the site’s authorities. The camp holds women and children thought to have traveled to Syria to join the Islamic State, although none of the women has been formally charged with a crime. The families spent their first night in a hotel. The general mood was incredulous disbelief, as many of the children saw beyond the walls of Roj camp for the first time. “Hands are not for hitting,” one mother gently chided her excited children. A few hours later, however, the women, all of whom are ethnically Somali, were moved to a makeshift underground detention facility, photographs of which have been viewed by New Lines Magazine . There, they were told that they would likely be getting deported to Somalia. The facility conditions appear dire: There is no heating, and the families have not been supplied with sufficient blankets. There is no ventilation or sunlight, and the white fluorescent lights are kept on 24 hours a day. They also have not been provided with clean drinking water. New Lines Magazine was shown a photo of a child whose lips were bleeding and chapped, apparently due to the lack of water. On Tuesday, several women went on hunger strike to protest the inhumane conditions. Then, on Wednesday, after the women refused to have their photos taken by Syrian officials, their phones were withheld for a prolonged period. According to one woman, the worst part of their experience so far has been the lack of clarity about what will happen to them. The women say they have not had any communication from the Somali government. They also say that the Syrian government has not given them concrete information, other than telling them that they are sorting out papers, and that it might take a “couple of weeks.” A representative from the Somali embassy, who declined to be named, said the women and children were not yet in the custody 
  https://newlinesmag.com/running-notes/somali-women-and-children-from-roj-camp-held-in-damascus/

* Alysa Liu Feels It All
  Longreads | 16 Nov 18:51 UTC
  The figure skating phenom took an unconventional path to Olympic gold. Now she’s once again upending expectations — by staying true to herself.
  FULL TEXT: There’s no wrong way into LIFE Magazine ‘s first proper new issue in almost 20 years, a relaunch powered by more than 100 contributors and lively with photo-forward features. There are moving interviews with Paralympic powerlifter Garrison Redd and Chris Bledsoe, a North Carolina man who communes with mysterious apparitions , each richly complemented by portraits (by Melchizedek Gayle and Walker Bankson, respectively). There’s the great Stacey Kranitz, snapping away alongside Mesha Maren in a dispatch from West Virginia coal country . Whatever your path, stick the landing with Bonnie Tsui’s profile of Alysa Liu, the Olympic figure skating gold medalist who, at 21, has shaped her own dynamic with her chosen sport by walking away when she must and returning when she wishes. (Nice photos, too, by some guy named Spike Jonze .) Women’s figure skating is the marquee event of every Winter Olympics.   FULL TEXT: Out of curiosity, researchers at a small artificial intelligence lab in Accra, Ghana, removed the guardrails on their drug discovery AI model in 2024. They asked it to design toxins instead. The model produced designs for 1,020 proteins and 5,000 small molecules. The researchers never made any of them in a lab, but software that predicts how poisonous a substance is scored all of them as likely toxic. Some of them resembled ricin, a deadly poison. The study was uploaded to the preprint server arXiv in May 2025. “We were all shocked,” Darlington Akogo, founder of MinoHealth AI Labs, told Rest of World. For Akogo, the results also raised a new question: If someone took designs like these to a company that makes DNA to order, would anyone stop them? Across Africa, the need to regulate DNA synthesis providers is now echoing through the highest levels of government, Brenda Obuya, a senior official at Kenya’s Ministry of Information, Communications and the Digital Economy, told Rest of World. Akogo has traveled the world warning that AI poses an existential bioweapons threat. He discussed it on the sidelines of the World Bank Group and International Monetary Fund Spring Meetings held in Washington, D.C., last year. He discussed the broader risk over dinner with Microsoft co-founder Bill Gates in 2023, whom he described as “on the fence” at the time. This year, Gates has issued numerous public warnings. “A.I. has crossed the threshold that its ability to empower a bioterrorist to kill hundreds of millions, that exists today,” he said on The Ezra Klein Show in September. The study has been discussed within Interpol, showing just how far it has reached. “I think the fact that the Ghanaian AI lab examined this and highlighted the potential for misuse shows that African institutions are contributing to … potentially to the governance [of AI],” Adam Abdullahi, a fellow at the International Biosecurity and Biosafety Initiative for Science, told Rest of World . LLMs have become even more capable since the study came out and the barrier for entry has lowered, Ayantola Alayande, a researcher at the Global Center on AI Governance, told Rest of World. “It wouldn’t be a surprise now to see even … [lay] users generate this sort of output via chatbots.” Scholars have long warned about AI’s bioweapons threat, but it is only recently that AI systems have truly outperformed Ph.D.-level biologists . * The horse made the state
  Aeon | 16 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Civilisation wasn’t made possible by grain, but by transportation – and the conquest it enabled, from the steppe to the sea - by Lhamsuren Munkh-Erdene Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: A horse rider at the market place in Urga (now Ulaanbaatar), Mongolia, photographed by Stéphane Passet in July 1913. Courtesy the Musée Albert-Kahn, France Transportation is the backbone of civilisation, which is why controlling the Strait of Hormuz grants global leverage. Because oil fuels more than 90 per cent of global transit, the source of this power lies not in the waterway, but in the control of global mobility. Halting all oil supplies paralyses virtually all transport, communication and supply lines: cities collapse, states disintegrate, societies break down and populations die off, throwing humanity back to the pre-industrial age. Without transport animals, humanity would still be back in the Stone Age. No city or state can exist without transportation, for there can be no concentration of population and resources. No transportation means no extrahuman transportation, leaving walking and carrying as the only means of transit. This baseline determined the size and structure of human communities. Production was limited to what could be carried home, circumscribing the areas where humans could settle and colonise. Production capacity and resource availability kept communities small, forcing them to split and disperse. Because size dictates structure, small meant undifferentiated and egalitarian. Ultimately, the inherent limit of the physical capability of travel and portage formed a natural immunity to inequality: it kept communities small, egalitarian and autonomous, leaving their lands beyond conquest. As long as this constraint existed, communities resembled those of the Amazon Basin or the New Guinea Highlands. The domestication of animals and the invention of boats liberated humanity, making long-distance mobilisation possible. With this newfound capacity, communities no longer needed to fit their immediate environments; instead, they could build cities and integrate expansive areas – all roads lead to Rome. Transportation – not grain – is what brought the state and civilisation into existence, and what continues to maintain them. Let’s examine this power by looking from the present back to the origin of the state. Contemporary live marine traffic map available at marinetraffic.com W hoever rules the waves rules the world! Without its navy and air force, the United States cannot command global reach or project power worldwide. The same was true of European colonial empires; ultimately, warships and gunboats forged them. In earlier periods, Viki
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-origins-of-the-state-are-not-agrarian-but-pastoralist

In some ways a close relative of the countless American films about U.S. emigres escaping violence in a strange foreign country, “Once Upon a Time in the Middle East” takes stock figures — an Arab who’s lived in and grown to love the hero’s land (in this case not America, but China), a local boy adopted by a transplant who’s moved to find work in a new country, a slick expat fixer with a heart of gold — and plays them against genre expectations in ways that reflect the anxieties and aspirations of an age of global Chinese soft power. The film evolves beyond the worldview of both its American predecessors and other popular Chinese movies that have gained international attention, at once portraying Chinese expats as facilitators of peace and genuine intercultural understanding while hinting at a more objectionable, paternalistic side to China’s engagement with the world. * Fugitive imagination
  Aeon | 13 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Wherever people are confined, displaced, silenced or made disposable, fugitive forms of knowledge and imagination emerge - by Shahram Khosravi Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Refugees at Moria Camp, Lesbos, Greece, July 2015. Photo by Georgios Makkas/Panos In 1986, I was 19 and behind bars in a small prison on the border between Iran and Pakistan. I had been arrested while attempting to cross the border irregularly into Pakistan. Everyone I met in that prison had been detained in connection with the border. It was a small space, the cells overcrowded and unsanitary, but it held an extraordinary diversity of people: local smugglers, large-scale drug-traffickers, undocumented migrant workers, and refugees; Iranians, Afghans, Pakistanis, as well as Baluch men whose communities had crossed these lands freely for generations but who were now being punished for violating the territorial order of the nation-state. Multiple languages were spoken at once. Different histories, geographies and ways of inhabiting the world converged within the same confined space. Yet we all shared one thing. We had refused, for different reasons, to respect the border. The state saw us through a single imaginary: as criminals, trespassers or illegal bodies to be contained. We, however, were animated by different imaginations. Each of us had imagined a life that exceeded the border and acted upon that imagination. In that sense, the prison did not simply contain border-crossers; it brought together people whose aspirations had collided with the territorial imaginary of the nation-state. That prison experience was perhaps one reason I became a scholar of borders. It remains, even today, the most important school of border studies I have ever attended. The conversations in the cells and during brief moments in the prison yard revolved around borders; how they operated, how they were enforced and, above all, how they might be circumvented. The prison became an unlikely site of knowledge-production: not simply the sharing of survival strategies but the enactment of an imagination that paid no heed to prison walls or state borders. M any years later, in a prison north of Stockholm, I interviewed a human smuggler who told me he saw himself as an architect: ‘I design a journey, like an architect designs a building,’ he said. The comparison stayed with me. Architects imagine and design structures that do not yet exist. Their work begins with the capacity to visualise what is not yet visible. In a similar way, smugglers design border crossings that, from the perspective of the state, would be impossible. A smuggler’s work consists of imagining routes where borders 
  https://aeon.co/essays/those-forced-to-cross-borders-are-imagining-a-radical-new-world

* An Ode to the ‘Space Jam’ Soundtrack on Its 30th Anniversary
  New Lines | 12 Nov 12:32 UTC
  Thirty years after the live-action animation comedy hit theaters, its soundtrack remains iconic, but has become increasingly hard to find on streaming platforms. The post An Ode to the ‘Space Jam’ Soundtrack on Its 30th Anniversary appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: One of the greatest movie soundtracks came out 30 years ago today, and it still stands the test of time. The 1996 movie “Space Jam,” the live-action animation comedy starring Bugs Bunny and the Looney Tunes, along with the greatest basketball player of all time, Michael Jordan, has become a cult classic seared into the nostalgic memories of millennials, despite the film being panned by critics at the time. In 2021, it even received a long-anticipated — and completely forgettable — sequel starring LeBron James. Yet even with the high-flying acrobatics of Jordan and the zaniness of Daffy Duck, Yosemite Sam and Tweety Bird, the standout of the movie is not even the film itself. The accompanying soundtrack, released by Warner Brothers and Atlantic Records, entered the cultural zeitgeist with a thundering boom in the mid-90s. The album is laced with banger after banger, spawning six singles — some released well after the film made its theatrical debut. The soundtrack felt contemporary, pulling together musical acts across R&B and hip-hop. Billboard even named it the fourth best-performing album of 1997 and one of the top 100 albums of the ’90s. Yes, there was the Grammy-winning “I Believe I Can Fly” by R. Kelly, though that is now difficult to listen to given the severity of his atrocious crimes. The British Empire attempted to invade Buenos Aires twice, in 1806 and 1807, while the territory was still under Spanish colonial rule, and was repelled both times. Still, from the late 19th century until the mid-20th century, the two nations maintained close ties: England bought Argentine food products and sold manufactured goods and railway infrastructure in return.   FULL TEXT: In Taoyuan, Taiwan’s bustling industrial hub where 200 semiconductor manufacturing facilities are based, Mayor San-Cheng Chang is using AI to orchestrate an ambitious transformation. Since March 2023, Taoyuan has run an AI-powered lung cancer screening initiative targeting citizens over 40 with risk factors that include occupational exposure and family history. Two years later, Taoyuan’s program had screened over 400,000 residents, detecting 233 lung cancer cases (90% in early stage), and achieving cost savings of NT$500 million ($17 million) through AI-aided diagnostics. To manage this volume without overwhelming local hospitals, the city deployed advanced AI software, which can predict cancer risk from a single low-dose CT scan without requiring additional clinical data, helping doctors to efficiently triage cases and focus resources on those most at risk. Modern AI-powered predictive models are being dangerously relied on in more decision-making arenas, including the delivery of government services, without justification. When residents complained about noise from illegally modified vehicle exhaust pipes, Taoyuan developed an AI system that analyzes exhaust pipe images and matches them against a database of legal configurations. Patrol cars equipped with cameras can now identify violations on sight, and complaints are down 20% over last year. Other AI-powered cameras monitor construction sites and factory chimneys for pollution, automatically triggering water sprays when dust levels rise too high, or alerting inspectors when chimney emissions change suspiciously. AI now optimizes traffic-light cycles at busy intersections, shaving about 10 seconds off each wait. * Why anything exists
  Aeon | 12 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Here’s a new answer to the old question ‘why is there something rather than nothing?’ (It’s neither simple nor easy) - by Andrew T Brenner Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Icebergs (1893-95) by Robert E Peary. Courtesy the Met Museum , New York Writing in 1714, Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz asked: ‘Why is there something rather than nothing?’ Philosophers have long wondered why the Universe exists, but Leibniz is credited with framing the issue in precisely these terms. The question is head-spinning when properly considered. This very obvious fact about the world – that things exist – can shift from seeming mundane and trivial to very surprising, even downright weird. Ludwig Wittgenstein expressed this astonishment when he wrote in his Tractatus (1921): ‘Not how the world is, is the mystical, but that it is.’ Arthur Schopenhauer in 1818 called the question ‘an unfathomable and always disquieting riddle’. And the Rigveda , one of the world’s oldest surviving religious texts, suggests that even the ‘overseer’ ( adhyakṣaḥ ) of the cosmos might not know the answer. Sheer existence is baffling. Philosophers continue to discuss the question today, but there isn’t any consensus about the answer. Some have suggested that it’s the sort of question that couldn’t possibly have an answer. In 1973, the German philosopher Carl Hempel wrote: His idea is that if you were trying to explain why, for instance, any penguins at all exist, then you couldn’t appeal to a penguin in your explanation – after all, that penguin is one of the things you are trying to explain. Similarly, if we’re looking for an explanation for why anything at all exists, then our explanation cannot appeal to an existing thing, since that existing thing is one of the things we are trying to explain. But Hempel thinks that there couldn’t possibly be any other sort of explanation for why anything exists, since any explanation for why something exists has to appeal to some other existing thing. One response to Hempel’s concern is to go ahead and do what he claimed can’t possibly be done: offer an explanation for why there is something rather than nothing, an explanation that ‘does not assume the existence of something’. Many philosophers have attempted to do just that. But there is something wrong with these explanations. W hy might we expect there to be an explanation for why there is something rather than nothing in the first place? Leibniz thought there must be an explanation for why there is something rather than nothing because, as he put it, nothing would have been so much ‘simpler and easier’ than something. It is this thought that lies behind much of the astonishment peo
  https://aeon.co/essays/a-new-answer-to-why-there-is-something-rather-than-nothing

Within months of Röntgen’s discovery, the unintended consequences of the X-ray began to emerge. In Tennessee, a boy came to the hospital after an accidental gunshot wound to his head. To test the equipment before trying it on his patient, his physician first conducted a prolonged X-ray of his own head. Three weeks later, the physician lost his hair. That same year, a New York doctor conducting X-ray experiments on himself noticed that his fingernails had stopped growing, his knuckles burned, and even the skin of his hand began to peel away. Blisters bloomed on other people’s bodies—on their hands and arms and chests. Thomas Edison started feeling cramps in his stomach; his vision began to blur. His glassblower, Clarence Dally, tested the quality of the fluoroscope tubes through daily X-rays of his own body. His hair started falling out, and his eyelashes and eyebrows burned away. His hands became red, swollen, and ulcerated. Knobs of deformed tissue bulged from his fingers. A biopsy of Dally’s pinky revealed cancer. . . . When Edison saw Dally’s tumors, he swore to never again expose himself or anyone else to radiation. Other popular anti-counterfeiting techniques used by high-end wineries include manufacturing proprietary bottle shapes and micro-printing, where text and patterns are printed at a scale visible only under magnification. Some wineries have adopted advanced security measures, incorporating features like holograms, individualised digital tracking codes and inks that change colour when tilted. I’m out of practice.” Above his head is the faded image of a mustachioed man wearing a dusty-looking tarboush. “It is from him that I learned to love storytelling,” says Shadi. We sat on Lau’s back porch, drinking tea. Halifax is a medical anthropologist, a Buddhist teacher and a caregiver to the dying, who has worked with the suffering of others for more than 50 years: in burn units and jail clinics, in remote Himalayan villages, at the bedsides of people for whom there was nothing left to cure. She learned that the qualities that drew her there could also pull her under, and more than once they did. When she found her way back, it was Buddhist practice that helped her remain steady enough to help. Halifax had almost died. She had gone into the operating room at Stanford cheerful, she told me – upbeat, confident. Surgeons repaired her heart’s mitral valve. Coming out of anaesthesia, intubated, she was certain the tube was positioned incorrectly. Then came what she called the worst 12 days of her life. The valve replacement was failing. Her blood pressure plunged; she bled internally; she had a dangerous arrhythmia, and had to be shocked to restore a normal heart rhythm. ‘Death is easier than this,’ she told Lau. She meant it. ‘I actually wanted to die,’ she said. ‘I just was so sick.’ She was still on a gurney from the latest resuscitative shock when the surgeon appeared in his scrubs and told her: ‘We have to take you back in.’ In the intensive care unit after the second operation, she awoke convinced she was hanging upside down against a wall of moss. A warmth enveloped her chest and expanded, and kept expanding, into utter bliss.
